Done. I consolidated everything into the final five-page Fable Sleight website and
refactored the existing pages instead of adding more redundant ones.
The final information architecture is now:

      Home - The Second Mind: the 64-page Ari + Crissy comic. Story is preserved;
      navigation is updated.
      System: the complete technical explanation-identity/personality, host,
      relationship, self, world/causal/social/belief models, memory, recollection, routing,
      tools, learning, authority, privacy, deletion, rollback. Frontier/status repetition was
      removed.
      Build: replaces Roadmap and absorbs Roadmap + Research + Lab. It now
      owns progress, Stage G/G+, the 16-phase destination map, frontier research,
      benchmark plans, evaluation methodology, and evidence.
      Capabilities: absorbs the old planned Product page. It has the six-world
      Capability Atlas-Perceive / Reason / Create / Act / Learn / Initiate-plus what

                                 🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                  362 / 456
    Fable Sleight eventually looks like for different users with their own named
    intelligences.
    Compare: remains standalone. Same-question comparison first, competitive
    landscape second, five plain-English differences, then an optional technical matrix.

I also rechecked the competitive positioning before freezing Compare. Pickle currently
centers a personal memory-based OS, Sentience explicitly centers a digital
version/model of the person, Rowboat centers a local AI coworker with memory, Pally
centers a connected personal assistant, Mem0 centers production memory
infrastructure, while Hindsight/Graphiti expose increasingly sophisticated memory
architectures. (Y Combinator) The page therefore says “not publicly claimed”,
never “they don't have this,” when internals aren't public.
I also ran the density audit you asked for. System is ~861 visible words, Build ~725,
Capabilities ~454, and Compare ~605. Deep details are progressively disclosed; the
comic averages about 76 words per page despite being 64 pages long. YC's design
guidance similarly recommends reducing competing attention and getting visitors to
the product's “aha” moment quickly. (Y Combinator)

Yes. I think this is the missing business layer.
The long-term Fable Sleight vision is too ambitious to wait for before charging
anyone. But the answer is not to build a random side-product just to make money. We
should launch a narrow paid wedge that is literally the first useful slice of the
final architecture.
YC's guidance lines up with that. They recommend launching once you have a real
“quantum of utility,” talking to users, and iterating rather than waiting for the perfect
product. For fundraising, YC also explicitly says investors want the market, customer,



                             🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                    366 / 456
traction, business model, and a credible roadmap-not merely a grand technical vision.
(Y Combinator)

My strongest recommendation: Fable Sleight Missions
Not a separate company or permanent sub-brand. Think of it as the first commercial
mode of Fable Sleight.
The pitch is extremely simple:

  Your projects shouldn't reset when the chat ends.
  Fable Sleight remembers what you're trying to accomplish, why decisions
  were made, what happened afterward, and what still needs attention.

Target one type of person first:
solo founders, researchers, engineers and highly technical creators juggling
multiple long-running projects.
Not “everyone.”
These people have a painful version of the exact problem Fable Sleight eventually
solves:

  ChatGPT knows this conversation.
  Claude knows another conversation.
  GitHub knows the code.
  Calendar knows deadlines.
  Docs know the plan.
  Email knows commitments.

  But the human is still
  the only thing connecting all of it.


That is commercially understandable today.
And unlike “personal memory AI,” it doesn't place us head-to-head with
Pickle/Oki/Pally on their strongest positioning.


What the 6-month product actually does
Keep v1 brutally focused around five behaviors.

                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                            367 / 456
1. Persistent Missions
User creates:

  Ship my startup MVP

or:

  Finish my research paper

or:

  Land a quant internship

Fable knows that conversations, files, research and decisions can all belong to that
same mission.
Then three weeks later:

  “Where were we?”

doesn't mean retrieving the last message.
It means:

  CURRENT GOAL
  Ship prototype by Oct 14

  WHAT CHANGED
  API choice changed
  UI prototype completed
  pricing undecided

  OPEN DECISIONS
  Hosted vs local inference

  BLOCKER
  authentication




                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                           368 / 456
  NEXT LIKELY ACTION
  finish auth before expanding UI


That's an actual product.


2. Decision Memory
Whenever something consequential is decided:

  DECISION
  Use architecture B.

  WHY
  Lower initial infrastructure cost.
  Faster to ship.
  Enough capacity for beta.

  ASSUMPTION
  <1,000 initial users.

  REVISIT IF
  Usage grows above threshold.


Later Fable can answer:

  “Why didn't we use architecture A?”

That's considerably more valuable than:

  “I found a chat from March.”


3. Mission-aware recall
This is where G+ becomes commercially useful.
The user says:

  “I got another opportunity from NVIDIA.”

Fable shouldn't just search for “NVIDIA.”

                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter   369 / 456
It should understand that this may connect to:

  career goal
       │
  ML trajectory
       │
  open-source work
       │
  existing commitments
       │
  current internship applications


The early consumer product therefore gives G+ a real reason to exist.


4. Weekly Mission Review
Once a week:

Here's what moved.
  3 decisions made
  2 assumptions still unresolved
  1 deadline moved

  You spent most of the week on:
  UI polish

  But your stated priority was:
  retrieval reliability

  You may be drifting from the plan.

  Review?


This is the beginning of Crissy noticing something the user hasn't explicitly asked
about-without pretending we've already built Phase 10 autonomy.


5. Outcome follow-up
A week/month later:

                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                               370 / 456
  “You chose B because we expected it to save time. Did it?”

The user answers.
Fable records the result.
Eventually:

  “You've made this kind of tradeoff four times. Want to look at the pattern?”

This directly advances the long-term Experience → outcome → future judgment
architecture.


What we deliberately DO NOT put in v1
This is important.
No attempt at:

    full digital clone;
    world model;
    social model;
    autonomous desktop control;
    voice-first ambient assistant;
    continuous camera/audio capture;
    autonomous coding agent;
    self-training;
    robotics;
    huge connector ecosystem;
    perfect proactive agency;
    complete personal-life ingestion.

Those remain the destination.
The MVP wins by doing one job unusually well:



                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                        371 / 456
   keep long-running work cognitively continuous.

YC specifically warns that early startups need a product a small group loves before
trying to grow everywhere. (Y Combinator)


Why this is particularly good for our architecture
The nice part is that almost nothing becomes throwaway code.

Paid MVP capability                             Long-term Fable architecture

Mission workspace                               Mission Graph

Decision record                                 Experience Ledger

Project history                                 Memory fabric

Intelligent project recall                      G+ recollection

Weekly review                                   Attention / Cognitive Workspace

Assumption tracking                             Belief + Prediction model

Outcome check-in                                Outcome-learning architecture

Research with sources                           Phase 4 information intelligence

Memory inspector                                Phase 6 Inspector

GitHub/files/calendar integration               Phase 7 capability fabric

Later proactive alerts                          Phase 10 agency


This is exactly what I want investors to see:

  EARLY REVENUE PRODUCT
          │
          │ is not a detour
          ▼
  TRAINS / VALIDATES THE SAME
  ARCHITECTURE
          │
          ▼

                              🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                           372 / 456
  FULL PERSONAL
  COGNITIVE SYSTEM


The current product plan already says Fable Sleight's consumer distribution should
inherit transferable capability from the flagship architecture rather than become a
permanently reduced chatbot.
The current roadmap also already contains most of the pieces this MVP needs-
authoritative memory, conversation, public research, Experience Ledger, Mission
Graph contracts, UI/Inspector, and later integrations.
So we're productizing the front edge of the roadmap, not creating another
roadmap.


I would change the current Fable product timeline
Right now our ratified roadmap effectively says:
Phase 7: first signed Windows local-ingestion alpha.
Phase 8: earliest credible closed alpha.
That's extremely conservative.
I would add a new product-track milestone-not a new ALICE phase:

Fable Sleight Revenue Wedge
after sufficient G/G+ + host-neutral UI capability exists.
Something like:

  ALICE

  Stage G
     ↓
  G+
     ↓
  host-neutral packaging
     ↓

  ────────────────────────────

  FABLE SLEIGHT

                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                              373 / 456
  MISSION PREVIEW

     ↓

  paid closed alpha

     ↓

  paid public beta

     ↓

  capabilities expand
  as ALICE proves them


That doesn't violate the flagship-capability rule.
Fable only gets capabilities already proven upstream.


Proposed 12-month plan
Given we're at the end of August 2026, I'd frame the investor plan roughly as
September 2026 → August 2027.

Months 0-3 - Build the commercial core
Do not chase hundreds of integrations.
Build:

    Windows app;
    host onboarding;
    mission creation;
    conversations attached to missions;
    durable decision records;
    manual/local file ingestion;
    research with citations;
    memory inspector;


                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                        374 / 456
    deletion/export;
    G/G+ winning retrieval path once qualified.

Recruit around 20-30 design partners manually.
Talk to every one.
YC explicitly recommends this kind of early manual customer work rather than
premature scaling. (Y Combinator)


Months 3-6 - Paid private beta
Add:

    weekly mission review;
    unresolved-thread detection;
    decision outcome follow-up;
    GitHub;
    calendar;
    one document source;
    basic conflict detection.

And start charging.
The milestone isn't:

  “10,000 signups.”

I'd rather show investors:

  “27 people use this every week and 18 voluntarily pay.”

That's far more informative.


Months 6-9 - Public beta
Now make onboarding self-serve.


                             🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                     375 / 456
Add:

      free tier;
      paid subscription;
      automatic updates;
      one-click mission import;
      stronger recollection;
      limited proactive suggestions;
      mobile/web companion if demand warrants it.

This is when we start measuring genuine conversion and retention rather than
founder-assisted adoption.


Months 9-12 - Expansion
Only after seeing what users actually love:
Maybe users care most about:
project memory.
Or:
decision tracking.
Or:
weekly reviews.
Or:
cross-project conflict detection.
Whichever creates the strongest retention gets expanded.
Not whichever capability looks coolest on our roadmap.
That is important.


Revenue model
Adjacent products give us a useful pricing band.



                               🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                   376 / 456
Granola's Business plan is currently $14/user/month. Motion Pro AI is
$19/user/month. Pally charges $25/month for Pro and $100/month for its high-end
assistant tier. (Granola)
So I would launch around:

Free
1-2 active missions.
Limited history.
Basic local intelligence.
Basic research.
Enough to experience the product's value.

Pro - $18/month
or roughly $180/year.
Unlimited active missions.
Full history.
Advanced recollection.
Weekly mission reviews.
Decision/outcome tracking.
Integrations.
Stronger reasoning models / included usage allocation.
Memory inspector + export.
I'd keep one paid tier initially.
No Pricing MBA nonsense with seven packages.


Cloud inference should not destroy our margins
Our local-first architecture can actually become a business advantage here.
Use local compute for things like:




                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                     377 / 456
  indexing
  memory formation
  retrieval
  classification
  mission bookkeeping
  light summarization
  privacy screening


Then use frontier models only when the task warrants them.
Granola explicitly argues that its device-based architecture reduces infrastructure cost
relative to heavier cloud-based approaches. (Granola) Pally similarly gates model
quality and usage through different subscription allowances rather than offering
unlimited expensive inference at one price. (Pally)
For Fable, I'd define an internal economic gate:

  Model + cloud infrastructure COGS should remain below ~25% of
  subscription revenue before we aggressively scale paid acquisition.

Not because 25% is magically optimal.
It's a guardrail.
If expensive inference rises:

  local model
         ↓
  smarter routing
         ↓
  included allowance
         ↓
  optional additional credits


rather than quietly losing money on power users.


What the revenue can look like
At $18/month:




                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                            378 / 456
Paid users                     MRR                        ARR run-rate

100                            $1,800                     $21,600

1,000                          $18,000                    $216,000

5,000                          $90,000                    $1.08M

10,000                         $180,000                   $2.16M


I would not put 10,000 paid users in a YC application as a forecast.
Use it to show economics.
The actual investor milestone should be something closer to:

  “Prove 100 people will pay for persistent mission intelligence, then prove
  we can reach 1,000 without retention collapsing.”

YC's fundraising guidance specifically emphasizes customer adoption, growth, and a
clear mechanism by which users become revenue. (Y Combinator)

  Users pay $18/month. Every successive ALICE capability-better

Why I wouldn't launch the other obvious wedges
Generic memory assistant: already crowded by Pickle, Oki, Pally, Personal AI and
memory infrastructure companies. Pickle is explicitly building around “one person, one
memory,” while Oki sells local whole-life memory/search/action. (Pickle)


                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                          380 / 456
Cross-app personal assistant: Pally is already extremely strong there and charges
$25/month for precisely that experience. (Pally)
Digital clone: Personal AI and Sentience occupy that conceptual territory, and it
undersells our source/host/self architecture anyway.
Decision tracker only: too close to HEBB's positioning and probably too narrow to
naturally absorb the rest of Fable.
AI notetaker: Granola and a dozen others. Terrible place for us to fight.
General coding agent: crowded and disconnects from the core thesis.


Mission continuity is different.
It uses our Mission Graph + memory + Experience Ledger + G+ as the product.
Those are already central to where ALICE is going.


One thing I would make even more specific
For the first 50 users, I wouldn't market:

  “AI for your whole life.”

I'd market:

Fable Sleight for Builders
  Keep your projects, decisions and goals continuous across months of
  work.

The first users I'd actively recruit are:
solo founders
researchers
engineers building side projects
graduate students
independent technical creators



                              🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                         381 / 456
They're more likely to tolerate an early Windows desktop application, local models,
rough edges and manual onboarding.
And many have enough economic value attached to their time that paying $18/month
is not absurd.
YC's pricing advice similarly notes that the earliest adopters often care more about the
benefit than price when the value is clear. (Y Combinator)

One product.
One subscription relationship.
Increasing value as the architecture matures.
Not five different startups.




                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                           382 / 456
There is one governance consequence we'd need to handle deliberately: the current
roadmap says the first signed consumer alpha appears around Phase 7 and the
earliest credible closed alpha around Phase 8. If we adopt this commercial strategy, I
think we should eventually amend the product track to add an earlier Revenue
Wedge / Mission Preview gate, while preserving the rule that Fable Sleight cannot
silently outrun ALICE's proven transferable capabilities. That would be a product-
roadmap change, not a change to the ultimate architecture.
I think this gives us a substantially better YC story than simply saying “the complete
product will take years.”

No - you absolutely do not need 100 users, revenue, or a finished Fable Sleight
product before applying to YC.
YC's current FAQ is unusually explicit: about 40% of companies they fund are
“just an idea,” and most accepted companies have no revenue. YC's
homepage currently goes even further: “We fund companies with no revenue, product,
or fully baked idea.” (Y Combinator)
So I would not delay applying just to manufacture 100 users.
But there is an important second half:

  Having no consumer product does not disqualify us. Having only a slide
  deck and a grand vision would make us materially weaker.

Those are different things.
And fortunately, we're not actually at “idea only.”

What we really have
The YC story should not be:

  “I have an idea for a JARVIS-like personal AI. Someday I'll build it.”

It should be:

  “I've already spent months building the underlying personal-cognitive
  architecture and validating its hardest foundations. The complete product
  is a long-term moonshot. I've now identified a much narrower commercial
  entry point that I can ship first and charge for while the full architecture
  continues to mature.”

                             🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                 385 / 456
That's much stronger.
Your current consumer-product architecture already defines local host identity,
persistent memory, host-specific learning, replaceable foundation models, skills,
personal state, inspection, deletion and eventual broader capability.
And the roadmap already represents significant engineering work before the complete
consumer experience: identity/authority, evidence, memory, conversational
orchestration, public research, experience/outcome capture, Mission Graph and the
later cognition/agency stack.
YC can distinguish:
pre-product
from
hasn't built anything.
We're the former.


Would having 100 paying users improve our odds?
Of course.
If two otherwise identical teams apply and:
Team A: idea + architecture + prototype
versus
Team B: same thing + 100 people paying every month
Team B has removed a major piece of market risk.
So yes, the absence of users is a missing positive signal.
But it is not some checkbox YC requires.
YC's admissions team specifically says they have to evaluate teams that haven't
launched yet, and YC's current FAQ says they intentionally fund extremely early
companies. (Y Combinator)
There are also YC student examples where companies applied with very basic
prototypes and few users, then spent the following year figuring out the actual
product. (Y Combinator)
So I would not think:

                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                             386 / 456
      0 users = bad YC application


I would think:

      0 users means the rest of the evidence needs to carry more weight.



What would concern me about our application
today
Not the absence of revenue.
This sentence would concern me:

  “We'll launch our first product in about one year.”

That sounds slow for software.
YC consistently tells pre-launch software founders to get something in front of users
quickly. Michael Seibel describes the MVP as the ridiculously simple first thing you can
give users to determine whether you're delivering value at all, and says the goal of a
pre-launch company is to launch quickly and get anyone interacting with it. (Y
Combinator)
Their essential startup advice similarly says to launch once you have a small
“quantum of utility,” then talk to users and iterate. (Y Combinator)
So I would change our commercial roadmap from:

  12 months → first users

to:

  12 months → meaningful paid product

with something much earlier before it.


What I want us to have by the YC application
Not 100 users.

  Here's actual persistent governed memory.
  Here's the research/evaluation pipeline.
  Here's what we're currently testing.

That tells YC:

And:
we know what we're selling before reaching the moon.


That's the key distinction
The YC application doesn't need:

  “We already proved the company works.”

That's what later investors increasingly expect.
YC exists partly because that proof often doesn't exist yet.
What YC needs to believe is:


1. You're capable of building something unusually difficult.
Our technical progress helps enormously here.


2. You understand something about the problem other people don't.
Our whole competitor audit + architectural work helps here.

3. This can become huge.
The full Fable Sleight thesis helps here.


4. You're not going to spend five years hiding in a lab.

Yes - your intuition is basically right, with one important correction:

  The first Fable Sleight should not try to be the intelligence. It should be
  the continuity layer that lets existing intelligences work on one persistent
  mission.

GPT, Claude, Gemini, coding agents, etc. can provide the reasoning capability that
Fable itself does not yet have.
But I would not make Fable “just an MCP memory server.” That version is
already becoming crowded, and MCP alone cannot guarantee the behavior we need.

First, sharpen what the initial product actually solves
It's slightly more sophisticated than:

  “Connect every chat together.”

We don't want every chat connected to every other chat. That would eventually
become the same context-overload problem all over again.
Instead:



                                   🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                              416 / 456
  Chats belong to persistent Missions.

For example:

 MISSION
 Build startup MVP

 ├── Chat with Claude
 │   backend architecture
 │
 ├── Chat with GPT
 │   pricing research
 │
 ├── Claude Code session
 │   authentication implementation
 │
 ├── decision
 │   use local processing for private data
 │
 ├── open thread
 │   pricing still unresolved
 │
 └── outcome
     auth approach caused deployment issue


Each AI session is temporary.
The Mission survives.
So when you start a brand-new Claude conversation tomorrow, Fable can provide:

 WHAT THIS PROJECT IS

 WHAT YOU ARE TRYING TO ACHIEVE

 WHERE THINGS CURRENTLY STAND

 IMPORTANT DECISIONS + WHY

 OPEN QUESTIONS

 CURRENT BLOCKERS

                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                          417 / 456
  RELEVANT PAST EVENTS

  WHAT HAPPENED RECENTLY


The model gets a small Context Capsule, not your entire six-month history.
That is much closer to the real long-term architecture.


And yes: Fable v0.1 should use other models as its
brain
Conceptually:

                   FABLE SLEIGHT
                CONTINUITY KERNEL

                         │

         ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
         │             │             │
         ▼             ▼             ▼

       Claude          GPT          other models
       reasoning      reasoning        reasoning

         │             │             │
         └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                       │
                       ▼

                FABLE MISSION STATE

                goals
                decisions
                history
                open threads
                outcomes
                evidence


That is actually a very good early product architecture.


                             🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                   418 / 456
We don't have to build:
coding intelligence
research intelligence
vision intelligence
frontier reasoning
formal math
computer use
yet.
Claude/GPT/etc. already provide much of that.
Fable provides something different:

  persistent continuity around whichever model you use.

And there's a really nice strategic consequence:


We can demonstrate model independence immediately.
A user could work with Claude today.
Switch to an OpenAI model tomorrow.
Fable still knows the project.
That gives an early tangible version of our long-term:

  model ≠ intelligence continuity

idea.


But MCP by itself is not enough
This is the important technical finding.
MCP absolutely fits this product. It was designed so servers can expose resources,
prompts and tools to AI applications. Servers can also be local or remote. (Model
Context Protocol)
Anthropic supports local MCP servers directly in Claude Desktop, and packages them
as one-click .mcpb desktop extensions. (Anthropic)


                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                         419 / 456
So this works:

  Claude Desktop
        │
        │ MCP
        ▼
  Fable Sleight
        │
        ▼
  Mission state


And Fable could expose tools such as:

  get_mission_context
  record_decision
  record_outcome
  record_open_thread
  update_mission
  search_mission_history
  create_handoff
  get_current_state


Great.


However, MCP has a fundamental limitation
An MCP server doesn't automatically see the whole conversation.
The MCP specification intentionally isolates servers: the host controls what context
reaches them, and servers generally cannot simply read the host application's full
conversation history. (Model Context Protocol)
Tools are also model-controlled. In other words, the model decides when to call
them. (Model Context Protocol)
So imagine this:

  Ari tells Claude:

  "We decided to abandon architecture A
  and use B because latency was too high."



                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                           420 / 456
If Claude never calls:

  fable.record_decision(...)


Fable may never know.
That's a problem.
We need reliable capture, not:

  “hopefully Claude remembers to call our memory tool.”


This means Fable needs a small application of its
own
Not an AI capable of doing everything.
A continuity host.
I would build:

Fable Core
Local background service.
Owns:

  Missions
  Decisions
  Threads
  Events
  Evidence
  Outcomes
  Context Capsules


Then Fable has multiple interfaces around that same core.

                         FABLE CORE

             persistent mission continuity
                          │
             ┌────────────┼────────────┐
             │            │            │

                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter   421 / 456
              ▼                ▼          ▼

        FABLE APP          MCP           API/SDK
           │                │
           │                │
        Claude API      Claude Desktop    future
        OpenAI API      Claude Code       clients
        other APIs      Cursor etc.


This is the architecture I recommend.


Fable App is not another ChatGPT competitor
This distinction is crucial.
The Fable app can look like a chat application.
But underneath it:

  User
    │
    ▼
  Fable
    │
    ├── Mission state
    │
    ├── Context selection
    │
    ├── Decision history
    │
    └── Outcome capture
    │
    ▼
  CHOOSE MODEL

  Claude
  GPT
  future model


So we're not training or building the reasoning model.
Fable says:


                               🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter   422 / 456
  “For this turn, here is what the model needs to know.”

Then sends:

  current user message
  +
  relevant mission context
  +
  important decisions
  +
  open threads
  +
  required evidence


to Claude or GPT.
The answer returns.
Then Fable observes the entire interaction, because it is the host.
That allows Fable to reliably identify:

  new decision?
  new constraint?
  mission updated?
  thread resolved?
  new blocker?
  important result?


instead of relying on an external client to remember to call MCP.
That is a major advantage.


It also solves the ChatGPT problem
Current MCP support isn't uniform across products.
Claude Desktop is particularly friendly to local MCP. (Anthropic)
ChatGPT currently connects directly to remote MCP servers rather than arbitrary local
MCP servers; private/local servers require additional tunneling infrastructure. Full
modify/write MCP support is currently rolling out mainly for Business, Enterprise and
Edu, while some other tiers have more limited capabilities. (OpenAI Help Center)

The critical detail is that we do not change Stage G now based on Mem0,
Hindsight, Graphiti, Sentience, etc. Stage G needs to remain experimentally clean.
What changes after Stage G closes is the exact G+ experimental program.
G+ will no longer be a vague “build smarter retrieval” stage. It will explicitly make our
architecture compete against the strongest ideas we found:

    hybrid semantic + lexical + entity retrieval;
    temporal retrieval;


                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                              455 / 456
    bounded graph/associative expansion;
    mission-aware salience;
    active unresolved-thread relevance;
    context-aware reranking;
    adaptive retrieval budgets;
    automatic recall vs deliberate recollection;
    context fusion/allocation;
    deep archival recollection where necessary.

And we test those mechanisms against the frozen Stage-G baseline rather than
assuming any competitor's architecture is automatically better.
The winner becomes ALICE's qualified recollection architecture.
Only then do we start implementing Fable Sleight's first commercial capability-the full
transferable Mission Graph/Cognitive Workspace experience-on top of that qualified
foundation.
So our current priority stays very simple:
1. Finish Stage G properly.
2. Do not start Fable backend engineering yet.
3. In parallel, we can refine the Fable Mission product specification, UX, user
research, and YC story.
4. Immediately after Stage G, formalize the competitor-informed G+ spec
before executing G+.
5. After G+ freezes the architecture, begin Fable Mission engineering.
That is still the cleanest path.
